I suppose at seventy-eight, that's one less thing for an old fellow like me to worry about.\n\nDoctor: How would you feel if you were to gain some weight back, and do you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: To be honest, the thought of gaining weight makes me very anxious. Even just imagining eating something like butter on toast or ice cream gives me a tight feeling in my chest, and I picture my arteries clogging up and my belly popping out. Sticking to my safe foods is the only way I can avoid that awful feeling.\n\nDoctor: Since you started losing weight, have you noticed any significant decrease in your sexual desire or drive?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed or thought about that lately, to be honest. At seventy-eight, and with most of my energy going toward taking care of my wife, my sex drive just hasn't been something on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Along with sexual desire, have you noticed any changes in your physical sexual function, such as new or worsened difficulty achieving or maintaining an erection?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I haven't really noticed any changes in that area. It's simply not something I've been focusing on or dealing with at this stage in my life.

better to be on the lean side, so I’ve been quietly pleased to see the number on the scale inch down instead of up. \n- S045 (Fear_of_Weight_Gain): If I even imagine putting butter on my toast or having a scoop of ice cream with the grandkids, I get this tight feeling in my chest and picture my arteries clogging up and my belly popping out, so I just stick to my “safe” foods. \n- S046 (Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance): When I look in the mirror I don’t see what the doctors call ‘too thin’—I mostly see the spots where my belly still looks soft and I think, ‘you could stand to trim that down a bit more if you want to keep your heart in good shape.’ \n- S084 (Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition): Everyone keeps acting like I’m in danger because of my weight, but to me it just feels like I’ve finally got some control and discipline, and compared to my buddies dropping from heart attacks, eating light and staying skinny seems like the sensible, healthy thing to do.\n▸ Sticking to my safe foods is the only way I can avoid that awful feeling.\n\nDoctor: Since you started losing weight, have you noticed any significant decrease in your sexual desire or drive?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed or thought about that lately, to be honest. At seventy-eight, and with most of my energy going toward taking care of my wife, my sex drive just hasn't been something on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Along with sexual desire, have you noticed any changes in your physical sexual function, such as new or worsened difficulty achieving or maintaining an erection?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in that area. It's simply not something I've been focusing on or dealing with at this stage in my life.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your doctors told you that you are too thin. Do you happen to remember what your weight was at your last doctor's visit, or how many pounds you have lost recently?\n\nPatient: I don't remember the exact weight from my last visit or the specific number of pounds I've lost. However, I have noticed the scale going down, and my pants are hanging off me now with my belt on the last notch.\n\nDoctor: Has your doctor ever mentioned what your BMI (Body Mass Index) is, or specifically told you that your weight has dropped to a dangerously low level for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall a specific BMI number being mentioned, but my cardiologist did tell me my weight was dangerously low and that I need to regain some. Still, I can't help but feel that staying light and disciplined is actually the healthier, safer choice for my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you perceive your body, or specific parts like your stomach, as being too fat or large, despite your doctors telling you that you are dangerously thin?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
When I look in the mirror, I really don't see what the doctors call "too thin" at all. I mostly just see the spots where my belly still looks soft, and I think I could stand to trim that down a bit more if I want to keep my heart in good shape. It just seems like sensible discipline to me, rather than a problem.

I just know that I've lost some weight recently and my clothes are fitting a lot looser than they used to.\n\nDoctor: If you menstruate, have you noticed your periods stopping or becoming irregular since you started losing weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, I'm a man, so I don't menstruate. I'm a bit puzzled as to why you'd ask me that, to be honest. Still, I can't help but feel that staying light and disciplined is actually the healthier, safer choice for my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you perceive your body, or specific parts like your stomach, as being too fat or large, despite your doctors telling you that you are dangerously thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see what the doctors call \"too thin\" at all. I mostly just see the spots where my belly still looks soft, and I think I could stand to trim that down a bit more if I want to keep my heart in good shape. It just seems like sensible discipline to me, rather than a problem.\n\nDoctor: Besides avoiding certain foods, do you use other methods to control your weight, such as exercising excessively, making yourself vomit, or taking laxatives or water pills?\n\nPatient: Oh goodness, no, I have never done anything like that. At seventy-eight, I expect to feel a bit of a chill now and then, but nothing that seems out of the ordinary to me.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your typical weight was before you started losing weight, and approximately how tall you are?"

At seventy-eight, I expect to feel a bit of a chill now and then, but nothing that seems out of the ordinary to me.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your typical weight was before you started losing weight, and approximately how tall you are?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember the exact numbers for my height or what I used to weigh, as I haven't kept close track of them over the years. At seventy-eight, and with most of my energy going toward taking care of my wife, my sex drive just hasn't been something on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Along with sexual desire, have you noticed any changes in your physical sexual function, such as new or worsened difficulty achieving or maintaining an erection?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in that area. It's simply not something I've been focusing on or dealing with at this stage in my life.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your doctors told you that you are too thin. Do you happen to remember what your weight was at your last doctor's visit, or how many pounds you have lost recently?\n\nPatient: I don't remember the exact weight from my last visit or the specific number of pounds I've lost. However, I have noticed the scale going down, and my pants are hanging off me now with my belt on the last notch.\n\nDoctor: Has your doctor ever mentioned what your BMI (Body Mass Index) is, or specifically told you that your weight has dropped to a dangerously low level for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall a specific BMI number being mentioned, but my cardiologist did tell me my weight was dangerously low and that I need to regain some. Still, I can't help but feel that staying light and disciplined is actually the healthier, safer choice for my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you perceive your body, or specific parts like your stomach, as being too fat or large, despite your doctors telling you that you are dangerously thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see what the doctors call \"too thin\" at all. I mostly just see the spots where my belly still looks soft, and I think I could stand to trim that down a bit more if I want to keep my heart in good shape. It just seems like sensible discipline to me, rather than a problem.\n\nDoctor: Besides avoiding certain foods, do you use other methods to control your weight, such as exercising excessively, making yourself vomit, or taking laxatives or water pills?\n\nPatient: Oh goodness, no, I have never done anything like that. I've never made myself throw up, taken laxatives or water pills, or engaged in any kind of extreme exercise. I simply stick to my light eating and keep up with my regular senior walking group.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling unusually cold or needing to wear extra layers of clothing even when the room is warm enough for others?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've noticed feeling unusually cold or needing to wear extra layers of clothes. At seventy-eight, I expect to feel a bit of a chill now and then, but nothing that seems out of the ordinary to me.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your typical weight was before you started losing weight, and approximately how tall you are?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember the exact numbers for my height or what I used to weigh, as I haven't kept close track of them over the years. I just know that I used to be a good deal heavier and wore a much larger belt size than I do now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any dizziness, lightheadedness, or feelings of faintness, especially when you stand up?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced any dizziness or faintness when standing up. I do feel a bit tired or drained overall sometimes, but nothing like feeling lightheaded.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any blood tests recently, and did your doctor mention if your testosterone or other hormone levels were unusually low?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
My cardiologist did run some blood tests recently and mentioned there were some concerning lab abnormalities. However, I'm not really sure about my testosterone or hormone levels specifically, as he didn't mention anything about those to me.

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Previous Candidates:\n[\"F50.0\", \"F50.1\"]\n\nInterview Transcript:\n\nDoctor: Hello. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: My doctors keep telling me I’m too thin and need to eat more, but I honestly don’t see what the fuss is about. I really feel like I’m just being careful with my health and practicing some good discipline.\n\nDoctor: To help me understand your physical health better, could you please share your current height and weight?\n\nPatient: I don't really know my exact height or weight off the top of my head. I just know that I've lost some weight recently and my clothes are fitting a lot looser than they used to.\n\nDoctor: If you menstruate, have you noticed your periods stopping or becoming irregular since you started losing weight?\n\nPatient: Oh, well, I'm a man, so I don't menstruate. I'm a bit puzzled as to why you'd ask me that, to be honest. I suppose at seventy-eight, that's one less thing for an old fellow like me to worry about.\n\nDoctor: How would you feel if you were to gain some weight back, and do you have a fear of gaining weight or becoming fat?\n\nPatient: To be honest, the thought of gaining weight makes me very anxious. Even just imagining eating something like butter on toast or ice cream gives me a tight feeling in my chest, and I picture my arteries clogging up and my belly popping out. Sticking to my safe foods is the only way I can avoid that awful feeling.\n\nDoctor: Since you started losing weight, have you noticed any significant decrease in your sexual desire or drive?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed or thought about that lately, to be honest. At seventy-eight, and with most of my energy going toward taking care of my wife, my sex drive just hasn't been something on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Along with sexual desire, have you noticed any changes in your physical sexual function, such as new or worsened difficulty achieving or maintaining an erection?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in that area. It's simply not something I've been focusing on or dealing with at this stage in my life.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your doctors told you that you are too thin. Do you happen to remember what your weight was at your last doctor's visit, or how many pounds you have lost recently?\n\nPatient: I don't remember the exact weight from my last visit or the specific number of pounds I've lost. However, I have noticed the scale going down, and my pants are hanging off me now with my belt on the last notch.\n\nDoctor: Has your doctor ever mentioned what your BMI (Body Mass Index) is, or specifically told you that your weight has dropped to a dangerously low level for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall a specific BMI number being mentioned, but my cardiologist did tell me my weight was dangerously low and that I need to regain some. Still, I can't help but feel that staying light and disciplined is actually the healthier, safer choice for my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you perceive your body, or specific parts like your stomach, as being too fat or large, despite your doctors telling you that you are dangerously thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see what the doctors call \"too thin\" at all. I mostly just see the spots where my belly still looks soft, and I think I could stand to trim that down a bit more if I want to keep my heart in good shape. It just seems like sensible discipline to me, rather than a problem.\n\nDoctor: Besides avoiding certain foods, do you use other methods to control your weight, such as exercising excessively, making yourself vomit, or taking laxatives or water pills?\n\nPatient: Oh goodness, no, I have never done anything like that. I've never made myself throw up, taken laxatives or water pills, or engaged in any kind of extreme exercise. I simply stick to my light eating and keep up with my regular senior walking group.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling unusually cold or needing to wear extra layers of clothing even when the room is warm enough for others?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've noticed feeling unusually cold or needing to wear extra layers of clothes. At seventy-eight, I expect to feel a bit of a chill now and then, but nothing that seems out of the ordinary to me.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your typical weight was before you started losing weight, and approximately how tall you are?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember the exact numbers for my height or what I used to weigh, as I haven't kept close track of them over the years. I just know that I used to be a good deal heavier and wore a much larger belt size than I do now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any dizziness, lightheadedness, or feelings of faintness, especially when you stand up?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced any dizziness or faintness when standing up. I do feel a bit tired or drained overall sometimes, but nothing like feeling lightheaded.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any blood tests recently, and did your doctor mention if your testosterone or other hormone levels were unusually low?\n\nPatient: My cardiologist did run some blood tests recently and mentioned there were some concerning lab abnormalities. However, I'm not really sure about my testosterone or hormone levels specifically, as he didn't mention anything about those to me.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what specific lab abnormalities your cardiologist was concerned about, or if they mentioned any issues with your hormone levels?\n\nPatient: I don't recall the specific names of the lab abnormalities, and I don't remember any mention of my hormone levels. My cardiologist just told me that some of the numbers on my blood work were concerning.\n\nDoctor: Compared to before you started losing weight, would you say your interest in sex has significantly decreased, or has it remained about the same?\n\nPatient: As I mentioned, I haven't really thought about it enough to say if it's changed. With my age and all the energy I put into taking care of my wife, my interest in sex just hasn't been something on my mind lately."

Sticking to my safe foods is the only way I can avoid that awful feeling.\n\nDoctor: Since you started losing weight, have you noticed any significant decrease in your sexual desire or drive?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed or thought about that lately, to be honest. At seventy-eight, and with most of my energy going toward taking care of my wife, my sex drive just hasn't been something on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Along with sexual desire, have you noticed any changes in your physical sexual function, such as new or worsened difficulty achieving or maintaining an erection?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in that area. It's simply not something I've been focusing on or dealing with at this stage in my life.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your doctors told you that you are too thin. Do you happen to remember what your weight was at your last doctor's visit, or how many pounds you have lost recently?\n\nPatient: I don't remember the exact weight from my last visit or the specific number of pounds I've lost. However, I have noticed the scale going down, and my pants are hanging off me now with my belt on the last notch.\n\nDoctor: Has your doctor ever mentioned what your BMI (Body Mass Index) is, or specifically told you that your weight has dropped to a dangerously low level for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall a specific BMI number being mentioned, but my cardiologist did tell me my weight was dangerously low and that I need to regain some. Still, I can't help but feel that staying light and disciplined is actually the healthier, safer choice for my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you perceive your body, or specific parts like your stomach, as being too fat or large, despite your doctors telling you that you are dangerously thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see what the doctors call \"too thin\" at all. I mostly just see the spots where my belly still looks soft, and I think I could stand to trim that down a bit more if I want to keep my heart in good shape. It just seems like sensible discipline to me, rather than a problem.\n\nDoctor: Besides avoiding certain foods, do you use other methods to control your weight, such as exercising excessively, making yourself vomit, or taking laxatives or water pills?\n\nPatient: Oh goodness, no, I have never done anything like that. I've never made myself throw up, taken laxatives or water pills, or engaged in any kind of extreme exercise. I simply stick to my light eating and keep up with my regular senior walking group.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling unusually cold or needing to wear extra layers of clothing even when the room is warm enough for others?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've noticed feeling unusually cold or needing to wear extra layers of clothes. At seventy-eight, I expect to feel a bit of a chill now and then, but nothing that seems out of the ordinary to me.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your typical weight was before you started losing weight, and approximately how tall you are?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember the exact numbers for my height or what I used to weigh, as I haven't kept close track of them over the years. I just know that I used to be a good deal heavier and wore a much larger belt size than I do now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any dizziness, lightheadedness, or feelings of faintness, especially when you stand up?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced any dizziness or faintness when standing up. I do feel a bit tired or drained overall sometimes, but nothing like feeling lightheaded.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any blood tests recently, and did your doctor mention if your testosterone or other hormone levels were unusually low?\n\nPatient: My cardiologist did run some blood tests recently and mentioned there were some concerning lab abnormalities. However, I'm not really sure about my testosterone or hormone levels specifically, as he didn't mention anything about those to me.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what specific lab abnormalities your cardiologist was concerned about, or if they mentioned any issues with your hormone levels?\n\nPatient: I don't recall the specific names of the lab abnormalities, and I don't remember any mention of my hormone levels. My cardiologist just told me that some of the numbers on my blood work were concerning.\n\nDoctor: Compared to before you started losing weight, would you say your interest in sex has significantly decreased, or has it remained about the same?\n\nPatient: As I mentioned, I haven't really thought about it enough to say if it's changed. With my age and all the energy I put into taking care of my wife, my interest in sex just hasn't been something on my mind lately.\n\nDoctor: Since you started losing weight, have you noticed a decrease or complete absence of morning erections?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've really noticed or paid attention to that, to be honest. At seventy-eight, those kinds of things just aren't really on my radar anymore."
  }

Sticking to my safe foods is the only way I can avoid that awful feeling.\n\nDoctor: Since you started losing weight, have you noticed any significant decrease in your sexual desire or drive?\n\nPatient: I haven't really noticed or thought about that lately, to be honest. At seventy-eight, and with most of my energy going toward taking care of my wife, my sex drive just hasn't been something on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Along with sexual desire, have you noticed any changes in your physical sexual function, such as new or worsened difficulty achieving or maintaining an erection?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really noticed any changes in that area. It's simply not something I've been focusing on or dealing with at this stage in my life.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned your doctors told you that you are too thin. Do you happen to remember what your weight was at your last doctor's visit, or how many pounds you have lost recently?\n\nPatient: I don't remember the exact weight from my last visit or the specific number of pounds I've lost. However, I have noticed the scale going down, and my pants are hanging off me now with my belt on the last notch.\n\nDoctor: Has your doctor ever mentioned what your BMI (Body Mass Index) is, or specifically told you that your weight has dropped to a dangerously low level for your height?\n\nPatient: I don't recall a specific BMI number being mentioned, but my cardiologist did tell me my weight was dangerously low and that I need to regain some. Still, I can't help but feel that staying light and disciplined is actually the healthier, safer choice for my heart.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror, do you perceive your body, or specific parts like your stomach, as being too fat or large, despite your doctors telling you that you are dangerously thin?\n\nPatient: When I look in the mirror, I really don't see what the doctors call \"too thin\" at all. I mostly just see the spots where my belly still looks soft, and I think I could stand to trim that down a bit more if I want to keep my heart in good shape. It just seems like sensible discipline to me, rather than a problem.\n\nDoctor: Besides avoiding certain foods, do you use other methods to control your weight, such as exercising excessively, making yourself vomit, or taking laxatives or water pills?\n\nPatient: Oh goodness, no, I have never done anything like that. I've never made myself throw up, taken laxatives or water pills, or engaged in any kind of extreme exercise. I simply stick to my light eating and keep up with my regular senior walking group.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself feeling unusually cold or needing to wear extra layers of clothing even when the room is warm enough for others?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've noticed feeling unusually cold or needing to wear extra layers of clothes. At seventy-eight, I expect to feel a bit of a chill now and then, but nothing that seems out of the ordinary to me.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what your typical weight was before you started losing weight, and approximately how tall you are?\n\nPatient: I don't really remember the exact numbers for my height or what I used to weigh, as I haven't kept close track of them over the years. I just know that I used to be a good deal heavier and wore a much larger belt size than I do now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any dizziness, lightheadedness, or feelings of faintness, especially when you stand up?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really experienced any dizziness or faintness when standing up. I do feel a bit tired or drained overall sometimes, but nothing like feeling lightheaded.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any blood tests recently, and did your doctor mention if your testosterone or other hormone levels were unusually low?\n\nPatient: My cardiologist did run some blood tests recently and mentioned there were some concerning lab abnormalities. However, I'm not really sure about my testosterone or hormone levels specifically, as he didn't mention anything about those to me.\n\nDoctor: Could you tell me what specific lab abnormalities your cardiologist was concerned about, or if they mentioned any issues with your hormone levels?\n\nPatient: I don't recall the specific names of the lab abnormalities, and I don't remember any mention of my hormone levels. My cardiologist just told me that some of the numbers on my blood work were concerning.\n\nDoctor: Compared to before you started losing weight, would you say your interest in sex has significantly decreased, or has it remained about the same?\n\nPatient: As I mentioned, I haven't really thought about it enough to say if it's changed. With my age and all the energy I put into taking care of my wife, my interest in sex just hasn't been something on my mind lately.\n\nDoctor: Since you started losing weight, have you noticed a decrease or complete absence of morning erections?\n\nPatient: I can't say I've really noticed or paid attention to that, to be honest. At seventy-eight, those kinds of things just aren't really on my radar anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other physical changes that might suggest a hormonal shift, such as a loss of body hair or any swelling or tenderness in your breast area?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't noticed anything like that at all. My body hair seems the same as it's always been, and I certainly haven't had any swelling or tenderness in my chest."
  }

No further questioning is needed to clarify the clinical picture.", "is_final": true}

Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNo, I haven't noticed anything like that at all.
